--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score -12.39) uses:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=19, slow turning) with 3 transitions: random to state 1 (p=0.90), random to state 1 (p=0.47), neighbors>8 to state 1
- State 1: GoAwayColor (color 2=green) with 3 transitions: random to state 0 (p=0.97), black floor to state 0 (p=0.75), perceives magenta to state 0

This is interesting - GoAwayColor from green (clr=2) means robots avoid the green half and tend toward the blue half. When on black floor (source area), they transition back to explore. This seems to work well for collecting items from one half consistently.

The key insight: the FSM makes robots go away from green walls, so they preferentially enter the BLUE side. Blue items decrease score (good), green items increase score (bad). The robot collects blue items and returns to nest.

Wait, let me reconsider. GoAwayColor from green would push robots toward the nest (red wall side) or blue side. When they're on black floor, they go back to exploring. This creates a foraging loop.

**Strategy for round 2:**
1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation** (~6 machines): Small tweaks to champion - adjust probabilities, try GoToColor red (toward nest), try different color targets
3. **Exploration** (~7 machines): Different topologies - 3-state machines with explicit nest-finding, source-finding, return behaviors

For the champion, the key elements are:
- Exploration state that transitions to GoAwayColor(green)
- GoAwayColor(green) that transitions back on black floor or random

Let me design variants:
- Try GoToColor red (clr=1) instead of GoAwayColor green for returning to nest
- Try 3-state: explore → go to black → go to red (nest)
- Try GoAwayColor green with higher probability of staying

Color codes: 1=red(nest wall), 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Key observations from round 2:
1. The champion (-13.34) is a 2-state FSM: Exploration(rwm=19) → GoAwayColor(green/clr=2). All transitions in both states point back to state 0 (the destinations were remapped - in a 2-state machine, D=0 from state 0 means state 1, and D=0 from state 1 means state 0).

Wait, let me re-read the champion carefully. In a 2-state machine:
- From state 0: transitions go to destination 0 (which is state 1, since D indexes OTHER states)
- From state 1: transitions go to destination 0 (which is state 0)

So state 0 (Exploration) transitions to state 1 (GoAwayColor green) with high probability (0.90, 0.47) and neighbor condition. State 1 (GoAwayColor green) transitions back to state 0 with high probability (0.97, 0.75, 0.85).

The key insight: GoAwayColor(green) makes robots move away from green walls toward the blue side OR toward the nest. When on black floor (source), they return to exploration. This creates a loop that biases collection from blue side (decreases score).

Top performers all use this 2-state pattern with clr=2 (green). The 3-state machines with an explicit GoToColor(red) for nest return performed much worse (-0.02 to -1.80).

The gap between rank 7 (-12.70) and rank 8 (-2.17) is huge - 2-state machines dominate.

Key parameter variations to try:
- rwm: 10, 15, 19, 20 all work. Try 5, 8, 12, 25
- The transition probabilities in the best: p0x0=0.90, p0x1=0.47-0.50, neighbor threshold=7-8
- State 1 transitions: p=0.97 (random back), p=0.75-0.80 (black floor back), p=0.85 (perceive magenta back)
- Adding white floor condition to state 1 (on white = in nest, go back to explore)

**Strategy:**
1. Survival: copy champion
2. Exploitation: fine-tune rwm, probabilities, add white floor transition to state 1
3. Exploration: try different neighbor thresholds/steepness, try 4-transition states

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The scores are tightly clustered (-12.60 to -13.86) - all 14 machines performed well this round. The champion uses rwm=5 (very low turning rate = nearly straight movement). Key observations:

1. **rwm trend**: rwm=5 best (-13.86), rwm=8 worst (-12.60), rwm=12 third (-13.26), rwm=15 second/fifth (-13.42/-13.10). Lower rwm seems better (straighter movement), but rwm=8 was worst. This is noisy. Try rwm=1, 2, 3.

2. **State 1 transitions**: The 4-transition variant with white floor (p=0.70) scored -13.42 (2nd best). The basic 3-transition variant at rwm=5 is champion.

3. **Transition probabilities**: The champion has p0x0=0.90, p0x1=0.47, neighbor threshold=8 w=11. State 1: p=0.97 random, p=0.75 black, p=0.85 magenta.

4. **All machines use same basic topology** - this is converging. Need to fine-tune rwm and probabilities, and also try some genuine exploration.

**Strategy:**
- Survival: copy champion
- Exploitation: try rwm=1, 2, 3, 4 around the champion; adjust probabilities slightly; try different neighbor thresholds
- Exploration: try adding white floor condition (in nest → back to explore), try different probability values for state 1 transitions

The key question: can we push below -14? Try:
- Very low rwm (1, 2, 3) for straighter paths
- Higher p0x1 (more frequent switching to GoAway state)
- Lower p1x0 (less random switching back from GoAway)
- Try neighbor threshold 5, 6, 7 instead of 8

## Reasoning

The scores are very tight again (-12.56 to -13.17), all worse than the champion (-13.86). The champion from round 3 (rwm=5, w=11) remains the best ever. Key observations:

1. **rwm=5 and rwm=1,2 all perform similarly** - the champion's rwm=5 is still best ever. The range 1-5 seems good.
2. **w=11 vs w=15**: Both scored -13.17 this round, so w doesn't matter much in that range.
3. **p0x1=0.47 vs 0.50 vs 0.55**: 0.47 seems slightly better.
4. **p1x0=0.97 vs 0.95**: 0.97 is better.
5. **p1x1=0.75 vs 0.80**: 0.75 better (last place had 0.80).
6. **p1x2=0.85 vs 0.90**: 0.85 better.

The champion hasn't been beaten. This round was noisier - the champion's score from round 3 was -13.86 but same machine only got -13.17 this round. There's significant stochasticity.

**Key insight**: The champion is already quite good. I need to:
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Try small perturbations that might find a better local optimum
3. Try some genuinely different topologies to escape if we're stuck

Let me try:
- Different p0x0 values (0.85, 0.92, 0.95)
- Different neighbor threshold (6, 7, 9, 10) with w variations
- Different p0x2 thresholds
- Try GoToColor(blue=3) in state 1 instead of GoAwayColor(green=2) - going toward blue walls
- Try a 3-state machine where state 2 does GoToColor(red=1) for nest return

The fundamental mechanism: GoAwayColor(green) pushes robots toward blue side OR nest. When they hit black floor (source), they go back to explore. This loop works.

Let me also try: what if state 1 uses GoToColor(blue=3)? That would explicitly navigate to blue side.

## Reasoning

Key observations from round 5:

1. **GoToColor(blue=3) was catastrophic** (+12.73) - confirms robots should go AWAY from green (toward blue/nest), not directly toward blue walls
2. **3-state with GoToColor(red) scored -1.02** - also bad, confirming 2-state is best
3. **Adding black floor transition to explore state (p=0.60) scored -13.34** - best this round, interesting
4. **p1x0=0.99 scored -13.10** - slightly better than 0.97 for random return
5. **p1x1=0.75 is better than 0.65 or 0.70** for black floor condition
6. **Neighbor threshold 8 with w=11 is best** (8/9/10 all worse)
7. **p0x0=0.90 seems best** (0.85 gave -13.02, 0.92 gave -12.90)

The champion remains -13.86. The stochasticity is high (~1.5 points variance). Key finding: adding a black floor transition to state 0 (explore) helped (rank 1 this round). This makes sense: if exploring and already on black floor (source area), immediately switch to GoAway(green) to avoid green side.

**New ideas to try:**
- Add black floor condition to state 0 with various probabilities (0.50, 0.70, 0.80, 0.90)
- Try p0x3=0.70, 0.80 for black floor in state 0
- Keep champion's exact parameters but add this extra transition
- Try p1x0=0.99 (slightly better than 0.97) with black floor in state 0
- Try white floor condition in state 1 (if in nest, go back to explore)
- Vary p0x1 around 0.47 (0.43, 0.45, 0.50)

The top machine from round 5 adds `--n0x3 0 --c0x3 0 --p0x3 0.6` (black floor → GoAway). Let me build on this.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Key observations from round 6:

1. **Champion (3 transitions in state 0) ranked 1st again** (-13.57) - the 4-transition version with black floor condition was consistently worse this round (-12.52)
2. **The champion's exact parameters remain the best ever** at -13.86
3. **rwm=5 is best** - rwm=1 and rwm=3 consistently underperform with the 4-transition topology
4. **4-transition state 0 with p0x3=0.75 scored -13.26** - slightly better than p0x3=0.6 (-12.52) but still worse than 3-transition champion

The results suggest we're in a noisy landscape near the optimum. The champion parameters seem robust. The 4-transition variant hasn't beaten the champion despite multiple tries.

**Critical insight**: The champion has been stable at -13.86 for 4 rounds. I need to try genuinely different things to escape this local optimum while keeping the champion. Let me think about what else could work:

- The champion explores (rwm=5, nearly straight) then GoesAway from green. The random transitions are key.
- What if I try **GoAwayColor from red** (clr=1)? That would push robots away from the nest wall toward the source.
- What if I try a **single-state GoAwayColor(green)** machine? Robots just constantly avoid green.
- What about **different LED colors** - could they matter? (LEDs help robots perceive each other's magenta LED - transition c=7 checks for magenta)
- The perceive-magenta transition in state 1 (l1x2=5) is interesting - magenta is the LED color of state 1. So robots in GoAway state can detect other robots in GoAway state and return to explore. Try different LED colors.
- Try **GoAwayColor(green) as state 0** (starting state), with Exploration as state 1

Let me try:
1. Champion (survival)
2. Swap state order - start in GoAwayColor
3. Try clr=1 (red) GoAwayColor
4. Single-state GoAwayColor(green)
5. Try perceive color 6 (cyan=state 0 LED color) in state 1 instead of magenta
6. Fine-tune p0x1 around 0.47 (try 0.43, 0.45, 0.50)
7. Try p1x1=0.70 (between 0.65 bad and 0.75 good)
8. Try w0x2=13, 14 (neighbor threshold steepness)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Key observations from round 7:

1. **GoAwayColor(red=1) scored 0.00** - catastrophic, confirms green avoidance is the right mechanism
2. **p1x1=0.73 scored best (-13.12)** - slightly lower than 0.75 worked better this round
3. **w=13 tied with w=11** (-13.02 each) - steepness doesn't matter much
4. **p0x1=0.43 scored -12.09** - worse than 0.47; p0x1=0.45 scored -12.79; so 0.47 is the sweet spot
5. **p1x2=0.80 scored -12.07** - worse than 0.85; p1x2=0.85 confirmed optimal
6. **Starting in GoAway state scored -12.51** - worse than starting in Explore

The champion (-13.86) remains unbeaten. The landscape is noisy with ~1 point variance. We've been stuck at -13.86 for 5 rounds. 

**Fresh ideas to try:**
- p1x1=0.73 showed promise; try 0.71, 0.72, 0.74, 0.76, 0.77
- The perceive-magenta condition (c=7, l=5) in state 1 fires when a robot sees another robot with magenta LED. Since state 1 robots have magenta LEDs, this creates social feedback. Try tweaking p1x2 around 0.85 (0.82, 0.83, 0.87, 0.88)
- Try a 4-state machine with a dedicated "corridor" behavior
- Try GoAwayColor from both green AND red simultaneously (using two color perception conditions)
- The neighbor threshold condition in state 0 (c=3, p=8, w=11) - try p=7, w=11 or p=8, w=9

The p1x1 parameter (black floor → return to explore) around 0.73-0.75 seems important. When a robot on black floor (source area) has high probability to return to explore, it spends less time in the source. But it needs to spend enough time there to pick up an item.

Wait - actually the black floor condition in state 1 transitions BACK to explore (state 0). So p1x1=0.75 means 75% chance per step of returning when on black floor. That's very high - the robot barely stays in the source. Maybe lower values would allow more time in source?

Actually, the item is picked up just by entering the black area. So staying longer doesn't help - what matters is HOW you enter (from blue side, not green side). The quick return to explore after touching source makes sense.

Let me try p1x1 around 0.70-0.77 more carefully, and also try different p1x0 values.

5. **p1x2=0.83 scored -13.19** - worse than 0.87
6. **LED color 4 (yellow) in state 1 scored -13.00** - worse than magenta (5)
7. **p0x2=7 vs 8**: similar performance (-13.14 each)

5. **p1x2=0.87 is confirmed best** (0.86 worst, 0.88/0.89 worse than 0.87)
6. **p1x1=0.73 confirmed best** (0.72 and 0.74 both -12.98/-12.95)
7. **w=11 best** (w=10, w=12 both -12.73 vs champion's -12.73 this round)
8. **p0x2=8 (neighbor threshold) confirmed** - w=11 optimal

The scores this round show the champion's exact params only got -12.73 (rank 9), showing high stochasticity. The champion was established in round 8.

For the final round, I need to maximize chances of finding something better. The key parameters that showed promise:
- p0x1=0.48 (vs champion's 0.47)  
- p0x0=0.88 (vs champion's 0.90)
- Combinations of these

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
